4.3.19 The following measures have been taken to improve the housing satisfaction level of the 
personnel of CAPFs:- 
(i) Enhancement of delegation of financial powers of DG CAPFs for execution of Major 
Works from Rs.10 crore to 15 crore and Minor Works from Rs.50 lacs to 1.00 crore.  
(ii) Enhancement of financial powers for Special Repair to DGs CAPFs from Rs.14 lakh to 
Rs.50 lakh and Land acquisition from Rs.34 lakh toRs.5.00 crore.  
(iii) Authorization to Technical Officers of Engineering Wings in CAPFs to exercise powers 
of Technical Sanction, Tender Acceptance etc. to execute works upto Rs.15.00 crore, at 
par with Technical Officers of CPWD.   
(iv) In order to maintain accurate inventory of housing stock as well as for allotment of 
accommodation of CAPFs, a common portal is being developed for online allotment of 
Departmental Pool Residential Accommodation to CAPF personnel on the lines of 
General Pool Residential Accommodation allotment (e-Awas). This is expected to bring 
in transparency in allotment process.

4.3.23 Regarding the reasons for stress among CAPF personnel, the MHA replied that 
studies/investigations have been carried out in the past for assessment of the factors responsible for 
causing stress in Forces and suggest remedial measures for the same. One such study wa s 
conducted by BPR&D in 2004. Further, the Indian Institute of Management, Ahmadabad (IIMA) 
did a similar study in 2012 for BSF & CRPF. Some of the measures taken to reduce stress and 
improve working conditions among CAPFs and AR personnel are as follows:- 
 

39 
 
(i) Transparent policies pertaining to transfer and leave of CAPFs and AR personnel. The 
hospitalization period due to injuries while on duty is treated as on duty.  Choice posting is 
considered to the extent possible after the personnel served in hard area. 
(ii) Regular interaction of officers with troops to find out and redress their grievances. 
(iii) Ensuring adequate rest and relief by regulating the duty hours. 
(iv) Improving living conditions for troops, providing adequate recreational/ entertainment, 
sports, communication facilities etc. Crèche facility is also provided at various 
establishments (where feasible) to facilitate the female employees. 
(v) Facility of retention of government accommodation at the place of last posting (for keeping 
the family) while posted in NE State, J&K and LWE affected areas (except State Capitals). 
(vi) Providing better medical facilities, also organizing talks with specialists to address their 
personal and psychological concerns and organizing Meditation & Yoga routinely for better 
stress management. 
(vii) Adequately compensating the troops deployed in difficult areas. 
(viii) Other welfare measures like facility of Kendriya Police Kalyan Bhandar (KPKB),  
scholarship for wards etc.  
(ix) Air travel to Non Entitled category posted/deployed in J&K. Air courier services have also 
been provided to CAPF personnel deployed in NE States, J&K and LWE affected areas as 
welfare measure. 
(x) Designating retired CAPF personnel as ex-CAPF personnel for better identity and 
community recognition. 
(xi) Promotions are released regularly to eligible personnel as & when the vacancies arise. 
Financial benefits under Modified Assured Career Progression (MACP) are given in case 
promotion does not take place for want of vacancies at 10th,20th& 30thyears of service.  
(xii) In order to provide better healthcare facilities to Officers and Jawans of CAPFs, “Ayushman 
CAPF” scheme has been launched in association with National Health Authority (NHA).   
  
4.3.24 The Committee notes that a large number of vacancies are existing in almost all the 
Central Armed Police Forces as well as Delhi Police.  The Committee desires that c adre-wise 
breakup of these vacancies may be intimated to the Committee.  The Committee strongly 
recommends that all vacancies in senior grades should be filled up on urgent basiss as this has 
a direct bearing on the decision making of the forces. 
 
4.3.25 The Committee recommends that the remaining vacancies in other  cadres may also be 
filled within a time frame so as to increase the strength of CAPFs and further enhance their 
capability.  
4.3.26 The Committee also recommends to undertake recruitment drives at approp riately 
located places on urgent basis so that the candidates do not have to travel long distances to 
participate in various stages of recruitment. This will help in saving funds an d also ensure 
easy availability of candidates. 
 
4.3.27 The Committee takes note that BSF has been directed to take up with the 
Administration of UT of Ladakh regarding the problem of medical testing and issuing of 
Domicile Certificate in recruitment of BSF. The Committee desires to know abou t the 
decision made in this regard. The Committee notes about the recruitment rally in 22 districts 
of Jammu, Kashmir and Ladakh Divisions and recommends that more such rallies sh ould be 
conducted in J&K, Ladakh and LWE affected districts to fill the vacant posts. 
 
4.3.28 The Committee notes that sensitization-cum-awareness workshops have been 

40 
 
conducted in few airports out of 64 airports where security cover are provided b y CISF to 
sensitize personnel on the special needs of the autistic passengers and equip them with the 
required knowledge, skills and attitude to handle them in the Airport. The Committee , 
therefore, recommends that sensitization-cum-awareness workshop on the special nee d of 
autistic passengers may be extended to the remaining airports.  
 
4.3.29 The Committee notes the initiative taken by CISF/Aviation Security Group (ASG) to 
make use of shoulder carried localised public address system and use of recor ded audio/video 
messages in Hindi, English and Tamil languages. The Committee recommends to exten d the 
facility of shoulder carried localised PA system (Public Address system) in the Airports which 
are frequently faced with issue of congestion in the „peak hours‟ caused on account o f 
bunching of flights, lack of space, etc.,  with recorded audio/video messages in other Indian 
languages. CISF personnel should also be sensitized about the regional languages so that they 
can communicate with the passengers regarding the requirements of pre-embarkation  
security checks for swifter clearance at security check points. 
 
4.3.30 The Committee observes that different practices are being followed at dif ferent 
airports. For instance at some airports, passengers are asked to remove belts and in other 
airport they are not. Therefore, the Committee, recommends, that a SOP may be f ormulated 
to create uniform system of checking and frisking at all airports throughout the country. 
4.3.31 The Committee notes with disappointment that women presently constitute only 
3.05% of the total posted strength of CAPFs. The Committee recommends to take special  
measures to increase  the representation of women in CAPFs. The Committee also 
recommends that the recruitment of women may be considered on the basis of operational 
requirement which need not be uniform across all forces. It should be done based on 
requirement and utility particularly in border-Guarding forces.  
 
4.3.32 The Committee notes that as a stop-gap arrangement, I-Sat phones were pr ovided to 
CAPFs personnel to communicate with their families. The Committee further notes that as a 
permanent solution for the problem, DSPTs have been re-activated at 719 sites from ou t of 
1047 sites with the help of Bharat Broadband Network Limited (BBNL).The Committee, 
recommends that the DSPTs at remaining 328 sites may be re-activated at the earliest so that  
the CAPF personnel deployed there can regularly communicate with their families and also 
use internet services. 
4.3.33 The Committee notes that the current housing satisfaction level of the CAPFs is 
45.74%. The Committee further notes the current housing satisfaction level of the Sashastra 
Seema Bal (SSB) personnel is as low as 26.17% followed by the Indo Tibetan Borde r Police 
(ITBP) personnel which is 39.93%. In view of the fact that still there is a shortfall of 1,45,602 
dwelling units for CAPFs, the Committee, recommends that sufficient budgetary allocations 
should be made to provide adequate accommodation to the CAPFs and ongoing con struction 
of 23,973 houses should be completed within a time frame. The Committee is of the 
considered view that it is the duty of Government to provide proper accommodation  and 
satisfaction to the Force personnel who are deployed at tough terrain with harsh climatic 
conditions.  
4.3.34 The Committee observes that the Defence Forces Personnel in battalions below 
Officers Rank (PBOR) are entitled for 30 days CL and CAPF‟s (i.e BSF, SSB, CRPF, ITBP & 
AR) personnel in battalions of all ranks are entitled for 15 days CL in a year. The Committ ee 
notes that CAPFs are getting less leaves as compared to the Army Personnel. They meet 
almost equal operational challenges, hardships and carry equal stress level. Therefore, the 

41 
 
Committee strongly recommends that the MHA may review the leaves given to the CAPFs to 
ensure that they do not feel discriminated and are abl e to spend adequate time with their 
families. This will also help in reducing stress among the CA PFs personnel.  
 
4.3.35 The Committee notes that just like Army Personnel, the CAPF personnel are also 
posted in the treacherous, mountainous and snowy terrains. Therefore, CAPFs personnel also 
needs to be given the Risk and Hardship allowances as admissible to Army personnel. The 
Committee, therefore, recommends that the MHA should take up the matter urgently on 
priority basis and extend payment of Risk and Hardship Allowance to the CAPFs personnel 
at par with the Army personnel deployed in similar topographies.  
 
4.3.36 The Committee observes that in a span of one year (2020), 134 persons have 
committed suicide in CAPFs.  In the CRPF number of persons who committed suicide  is the 
highest i.e. 53.  The Committee strongly recommends  that a proper study should be 
conducted to analyse the reasons as to why the Force Personnel are taking such drastic  step.  
Moreover, facilities like adequate leave, communication, better treatment of ailing family 
members, quick disbursement of pensions and „Risk and Hardship Allowances”, etc. should 
be provided to the CAPF personnel as incentives which will help to reduce their stress level to 
a great extent. 
 
4.4 Railway line between Bilaspur (H.P) & Leh 
  
4.4.1 The Committee was informed by MHA that the construction of railway line between 
Bilaspur (Himachal Pradesh) to Leh will be strategically beneficial to troops deployed in the area , 
as it will open up an alternative connectivity and will facilitate mobility of troops and logistic 
support. In so far as UT of Ladakh is concerned, once the said railway is operational, it will lessen 
the movement of load carriers and passenger vehicles presently used for transport of goods and 
movement of people. This can contribute to a smaller carbon footprint thus fulfilling the objective 
of a Carbon Neutral Ladakh. 
 
4.4.2 Railway line between Bilaspur-Leh is identified as a strategic line and final location survey 
(FLS) is being done by Ministry of Railways (MoR) on the request of Ministry of Defence (MoD ). 
FLS is funded by MoD. FLS is targeted to be completed by December 2021 by MoR. Project is yet 
to be sanctioned by MoD. A view will be taken by MoD after completion of FLS by MoR.  
4.4.3 The Committee takes note of the project of railway line between Bilaspur (H.P) & Leh. 
The Committee also understands the importance of the strategic railway line which will 
improve the mobility of troops and logistic support to the borders. Therefore, the Committee 
recommends that the MHA may take up the matter with Ministries of Railways (MoR) and 
Defence (MoD) so that final decision is taken on the project. This is certainly going to help i n 
facilitating the movement of troops, providing timely logistical support at borders. It will also 
help in promoting tourism in the Himalayan region.

4.5.5 The Committee was informed that against the sanctioned strength of 94,350 personnel, 
Delhi Police currently has 80,029 personnel and 14,321 posts are lying vacant. Further, the housing 
satisfaction level of Delhi Police personnel is 19.53%.  After taking over the housing projects in 
pipe-line, the housing satisfaction level will rise to 21.8%.  

43 
 
4.5.6 The expenditure for modernization of Delhi Police is incurred from the budget allocated 
under various Capital & Revenue heads of Delhi Police.  However, no separate fund has been  
allocated under 'Modernization' during the financial year 2020-21 and 2021-22. Apart from 
modernization work/ activities carried out from regular budget as mentioned above, financial 
assistance is provided to Delhi Police under various schemes, which inter-alia include the 
following: 
 
1. Safe City Project for Delh i: Approval has been accorded  for “Safe City Project” for Delhi 
with a total cost of Rs. 857.83 crore, to be financed from Nirbhaya Fund (Rs. 617.71 crore) 
and regular budget of Delhi Police (Rs. 240.11 crore). The project aims to create a safe,  
secure and empowering environment for women in public places. The 
infrastructure/procurement plan under the project inter alia include installation of 10,000 
CCTV Cameras in areas frequented by women, setting up of command and control at PHQ, 
district HQ and police stations etc.  
 
2. Cyber Security : National Cyber Forensic Laboratory (NCFL) has been strengthened 
through the assistance of MHA as under: 
 
(a) Image & Video Enhancement Lab: A forensic lab with 5 different tools has been 
established for enhancing poor quality images and videos, which will help in 
identifying suspects, vehicles, etc. Funds to the tune of Rs.2.94 crore were 
provisioned by MHA and the Lab became functional in March, 2020. 
 
(b) Malware Forensic Lab: A forensic lab with 2 different tools has been established for 
analyzing malicious applications, codes, URLs etc., which will help in tracing 
malware source, spyware destination, affected systems etc. Funds to the tune of 
Rs.3.8 crore were provisioned by MHA and the Lab became functional in June, 
2020. 
 
(c) Crypto Currencies Analytics Lab: A forensic lab with 2 crypto currency analytics 
tools has been established for analyzing crypto-transactions, wallets, exchanges, etc., 
which will help in tracing Bitcoin transactions, associated Wallets, Tumblers, 
linked-IPs, etc. Funds to the tune of Rs.79 lakh were provisioned by MHA and th e 
Lab became functional in March, 2020. 
 
(d) Online Memory and Mobile Phone Forensics Lab: MHA has accorded 
Administrative Approval in Nov.2020 for procurement of an Online Forensic Lab 
through which an Investigating Officer can access forensic tools from a remote 
location. Funds to the tune of Rs.14.5 crore were provisioned by MHA and the Lab 
will become functional by March, 2021. 
 
3. Nirbhaya Fund: New building with women centric facilities for Special Police Unit for 
Women & Children (SPUWAC) and Special Police Unit for North East Region (SPUNER) 
at Nanakpura at a cost of Rs.23.53 crore, being funded under „Nirbhaya Fund‟ is under 
construction. The building is expected to be completed by May, 2021. 
 
4.5.7 To increase surveillance of dark areas with no/insufficient lighting in the city which are 
potential areas of crimes especially, against women, Delhi Police informed the Committee that 
surveillance is being regularly done by the PCR Unit of Delhi Police patrolling staff.  They give  
feedback regarding the prevailing conditions of dark stretches/areas with no/insufficient lighting in 
the city through regular patrolling. After collecting data from all Zones, the information is sent to 

44 
 
the concerned officials on daily basis by Central Police Control Room (CPCR). 
 
4.5.8 The Committee notes that the current housing satisfaction level of Delhi Police 
personnel is only 19.53%. In the absence of proper accommodation, Delhi Police p ersonnel 
are residing in adjoining areas of Faridabad, Gurugram, etc. It delays response in em ergency 
situations. The Committee, therefore, recommends that the Ministry of Home Affairs shoul d 
take up the matter with Delhi Police and make yearly targets to increase number of 
accommodations for Police personnel. Adequate budgetary provisions should be made for the 
same. The Committee would like to be apprised about the progress made in this regard.

Adequate budgetary allocation may be requested to the Ministry of Finance for the same.

4.7.2 This provision is for construction of Residential and Office Buildings by Central Armed

4.7.3 The Committee was informed by MHA that in order to contain the spread of COVID-19 
pandemic, the Government had put in place comprehensive restrictions on all activities including 
movement of persons, goods, constructions etc., from the very beginning of the Financial Year 
2020-21. The restrictions imposed have resulted in disruptions in supply chain, availability of 
labour force, and other activities related to construction works. Though some of the construction 
activities were allowed from the month of May, 2020, the overall response was lukewarm. The 
construction activities could be resumed to a large extent only during the second half of the current 
Financial Year i.e. from the months of October, 2020 onwards. Hence, the implementing agencies 
entrusted with the construction activities could not achieve much progress in terms of expenditure. 
Therefore, in view of the absorption capacity of various agencies, the total budget provision was 
reduced.  
 
4.7.4 In order to use resources prudently in accordance with emerging and changing priorities in 
the wake of COVID-19 and unprecedented demand on public financial resources, new proposals 
have not been initiated/approved in the Financial Year 2020-21. Further, in compliance of modified 
exchequer control based expenditure management guidelines issued by Ministry of Finance, CAPFs 
were advised to restrict their expenditure within a cap of 10% of BE 2020-21 in the first and 2 nd 
quarter, except for Cost of Ration and Salary on actual basis. Further, due to COVID-19 pandemic  
and enforced lockdown, there was shortage of men and material across the country during the 
current financial year resulting in less expenditure for infrastructure projects. This was also one of 
the main reasons for reduction of allocation in RE 2020-21. The details of utilization of funds 
during 2019-20 and 2020-21 (till 31.12.2020) are as under:

4.7.5 During the next financial year 2021-22, for CAPFs, it is proposed to construct 150 barracks, 
169 office buildings, 13 hospitals and 7,765 residential quarters. The construction of CAPFIMS is 
going on, cost of which is also proposed to be met from this budget. Also, during the next financial 
year 2021-22, it is proposed to enhance the Office Building and Residential Building in respect of 
Sardar Vallabhbhai Patel National Police Academy (SVP NPA), Hyderabad  & North Eastern 
Police Academy (NEPA) , Shillong.

4.7.6 Besides, under Delhi Police Building Programme, 16 building projects are under

and Extra-Ordinary Repair (EOR) of buildings have been included in Police Infrastructure 
Umbrella Scheme during 2021-22 to 2025-26 for appraisal/approval of competent authority. Delhi 
Police will utilize the fund allocated in the year 2021-22 for the purposes  of 27 Building Projects 
(under construction & planning/ tender stage); 54 New Projects along with 48 land procurement 
cases; Extra-Ordinary Repair (EOR); and towards payment to DDA. 
  
4.7.7 The Committee notes that due to COVID-19 Pandemic, there is low utilization of 
funds during FY 2020-21. The Committee recognizes the paramount need for good 
infrastructure and facilities for police forces to enhance the quality of policing. Th e 
Committee observes that the requirements for the current fiscal year 2021- 22 under „Police 
Infrastructure‟ are massive as elaborated above by the Ministry. Particularly, the projects for 
CAPFs, constructions of CAPFIMS and projects under Delhi Police Building Progra mme 
require huge allocation. The Committee, therefore, expresses its strong concern that the 
reduced allocation to Rs. 3,612.29 crore in BE 2021-22 from Rs. 4,134.61 crore in BE 2020-21 
will be inadequate and hamper these projects. The Committee, therefore, recommends that 
the Ministry of Home Affairs should take up the issue with the Ministry of Finance for highe r 
allocation under this head in RE 2021-22. At the same time, the Committee also cautions that 
optimal utilization of the allocated funds be ensured in the ensuing year. The  Committee also 
recommends that the Ministry should speed up the construction work that came to halt due  
to COVID-19 Pandemic and complete the projects within a time frame without cost overruns.

2021 and the last in December, 2022.

4.8.3 The Committee observes that NATGRID was allocated Rs. 50.82 crore in BE 2019- 20 
and 52.17 crore in BE 2020-21 but only 23.93 crore (till 31 st January, 2020) and Rs. 28.09 
crore (till 31 st January, 2021) was spent in 2019-20 and 2020-21, respectively. It shows that 
the allocated amounts are not being utilized completely. The Committee notes that first phase 
of NATGRID has been started and second phase is under development. The Com mittee 
recommends that the Ministry should make realistic projections and also closely monitor the 
fund utilization under NATGRID so that the funds are not surrendered at the end of the year.

Nacrotics Control Bureau (NCB) 
 
4.9.3 The Committee was informed by the MHA that drug law enforcement agencies in India in 
coordination with the NCB are making efforts for interdiction in instances of use of dark net for 
drug trafficking in India. NCB has submitted a proposal to establish a Cyber Wing also having   
Intelligence Fusion Centre for effective detection and investigation of drug trafficking cas es 
involving crypto-currencies and Dark Net. However, with the available resources NCB has effected 
following seizures of drugs where crypto currencies and Dark Net were used by the traffickers. 
 
4.9.4 Narcotics Control Bureau has taken several measures and steps at national as well as 
international front to contain drug trafficking in the country. Some of these are: 
 
Regional/National Level: 
 
(a) For coordination between various Central and State Agencies, a new platform namely 
Narcotics Coordination (NCORD) has been established by MHA to conduct regular 

48 
 
meetings with different drug law enforcement agencies in India vide MHA order dated 
29.07.2019.  
(b) To monitor the investigation of drug seizures, the MHA has set up a JCC (Joint 
Coordination Committee) at Central and State level vide MHA order dated 19.07.2019.  
(c) For digitization and sharing of pan-India drug seizure data, the MHA has launched an e-
portal called „SIMS‟ (Seizure Information Management System) for all the drug law 
enforcement agencies under the mandate of Narcotic Drugs and Psychotropic Substances 
Act, 1985. 
(d) The NCB participates with drug law enforcement agencies, intelligence agencies an d 
Border Guarding Forces through MAC/SMAC, LIA and REIC platform for sharing of 
intelligence inputs and cooperation.  
(e) To keep check at the land and sea borders, Border Security Force, Sashastra Seema Bal, 
Indian Coast Guard, Railway Protection Force and NIA have been empowered under the 
Narcotics Drugs &Psychotropic Substances Act for making interdiction of narcotic drugs in 
remotest and far furlong areas of the country. 
(f) The NCB organized annual meeting of central agencies and states to formulate Action Plan 
for illicit cultivation in the country. With assistance of ADRIN, the satellite imagery are 
provided to concerned states to carry out destruction of such illicit crop.  
 
International Level
: 
 
(a) India is signatory to all the three conventions held on 1961, 1971 and 1988 in connection 
with Illicit trafficking of Narcotic Drugs and Psychotropic substances. 
 
(b) India has signed Bilateral Agreements (27) Memorandum of Understandings (15) and 
Agreements on Security Cooperation (02) with different countries for combating illicit 
trafficking of NDPS and Chemical Precursors as well as related offences. 
 
(c) India is closely associated with International Narcotics Control Board (INCB) and all its 
program viz. PEN (Pre-Export Notification) and PICS (Precursors Incident Communication 
System). 
 
(d) The NCB is organizing and participating the DG Level talks with Myanmar, Bangladesh, 
Sri Lanka, Indonesia, Afghanistan, Pakistan & Singapore to discuss and resolve the various 
drug related issues.  
 
(e) NCB co-ordinates with various international organizations such as SAARC Drug offences 
monitoring desk (SDOMD), Brazil,  Russia,  India, China  and South Africa  (BRICS), 
Colombo Plan, Association of Southeast Asian Nations (ASEAN), ASEAN Senior Officials 
on Drug Matters (ASOD), Bay of Bengal Initiative For Multi-Sectoral Technical and 
Economic Co-Operation (BIMSTEC), Shanghai Council Cooperation (SCO),   United 
Nations Office on Drugs and Crime (UNODC), International Narcotics Control Board 
(INCB) etc for sharing information and intelligence to combat trans-national drug 
trafficking. 
 
4.9.5 NCB India takes part in real time information sharing with various Drug Liaison Officers of 
other countries such as Drug Enforcement Agency (DEA) of United States of America, National 
Crime Agency of United Kingdom, RCMP of Canada, AFP of Australia, OFAST of France, etc.; 
for operational and intelligence information.  
 

49 
 
4.9.6 The Committee notes the steps taken by the Narcotics Control Bureau (NCB) at 
national as well as international front to contain drug trafficking in the country. The 
Committee expresses its concern about increasing use of crypto-currencies an d "darknet" for 
drug trafficking.  Further, technology is being leveraged by drug traffickers to maintain 
anonymity and making it difficult to track the movement of drugs. The Committee 
recommends that the MHA may advise NCB to establish a Cyber Wing with Intelligence 
Fusion Centre for effective detection and investigation of drug trafficking cases involvin g 
crypto-currencies and Dark Net at the earliest. The Committee may be apprised ab out the 
progress made in this regard. 
 
National Investigation Agency (NIA)
 
 
4.9.7 The Committee was informed by the representative of NIA that the allocation made to NIA 
is sufficient to meet its need. Regarding the vacancy, the Committee was informed that the 
vacancies has been a continuous problem in NIA. Efforts are being made to bring more people on 
deputation to NIA by providing adequate accommodation and additional allowance of 20% to its 
staff. The Committee was further informed by NIA that it has a total sanctioned strength of 1277 
posts, out of which 144 posts were created in September, 2020. Presently 427 posts (including 
newly created 144 posts) are vacant. The NIA being an evolving organization, the vacancy position 
is always dynamic. State DGPs and DsG of CAPFs are regularly approached through fo rmal 
channels to sponsor eligible and willing personnel for posting in NIA. 
4.9.8 The Committee notes that the vacancies have been a continuous problem in NIA. 
Currently, 427 posts are lying vacant which is 33.43% of the sanctioned strength of 1,277 
posts. The Committee further notes that the efforts are being made to bring more pe ople on 
deputation to NIA. The Committee believes that the vacancies in NIA are of serious conce rn 
as it is a premier investigation agency of the country. The Committee, therefore, recommends, 
that the vacant posts may be filled within a timeframe so that the investigation processes o f 
terror-crimes do not suffer due to shortage of staff. 
 Bureau of Immigration (BOI) 
 
4.9.9 In response to the query on the status of Marine Immigration check posts in India, the 
Ministry informed the Committee that at present there are 31 authorized Seaport Immigration 
Check Posts (ICPs) in the country out of which Immigration of 10 ICPs are under direct control of 
Bureau of Immigration (BOI), MHA and the remaining ICPs are manned by State Police personnel. 
Government of India has been promoting cruise operations through major Indian seaports. Though 
focus has been on facilitating foreign tourists, adequate matching efforts have not been made by the 
Port Trust authorities concerned to provide basic minimum facilities for professional Immigration 
services to the tourists inspite of the issue having been repeatedly taken up by BOI. 
 
4.9.10 Cochin seaport has been attracting cruise vessels since 2001 and the number of cruise 
vessels calling at the ports increased tremendously (2001=10 vessels, 2018=49 vessels). Similarly 
cruise traffic is expected to increase at all Seaports. There are difficulties in berthing ve ssels having 
length over 260 metres. Moreover, there are no infrastructure facilities available to handle the 
traffic of large vessels at Ernakulum wharf. Immigration authorities are expected to work in 
temporary structures/'Shamianas' set up by the Port Trusts, at the cost of the health of officials 
deployed there while exposing technical gadgets to inclement weather. BOI requires closed climate 
controlled environment along with leased line connectivity to conduct its operations. 
 

50 
 
4.9.11 At some ports, the foreign crews disembarking from the ships have to be taken outside the 
docks' secured areas without Immigration clearance. This is an undesirable situation. Every 
foreigner, upon arrival, as per security protocols is required to go through Immigration first as per 
procedure through access controlled counters. The offices of Immigration at many Seaports are 
however in dilapidated conditions. Though it was agreed upon to shift the office of Chennai 
Seaport to the Cruise buildings closer to the Immigration counters, however no action has been 
taken as yet. This temporary set up for Immigration creates difficulties for the transportation of 
technical gadgets, installation of the systems and programming etc. Due to lack of network cables 
and connectivity, it is difficult to use the makeshift counters for processing e-landing cards and 
passenger clearance inside the terminal and in the gangway. 
 
 4.9.12 It was further informed that the Seaport ICPs namely JNPT Mumbai, Mumbai Seaport, 
Kolkata, Vizhinjam, Vallarpadam, Cochin, Calicut, Chennai Mormugao & New Mangalore have 
inadequate infrastructure in terms of space/building for smooth Immigration function. Further, 
Immigration functions are carried out using ICT Technology which requires secure network 
connectivity through leased circuits and various Technical equipments viz. Servers, Computers, 
Passport Reading Machines, Barcode Readers etc. The ICT infrastructure requires appropriate 
housing in a Server Room with AC environment for proper functioning. The LAN (Local Area 
Networks) with Immigration Server Room and Immigration counter has to be properly laid out for 
smooth functioning. It is imperative that appropriate housing and infrastructure are made available 
for technical set up of Immigration infrastructure. 
 
4.9.13 The condition of the other 21 Seaport ICPs which are manned by the State Govt. personnel 
is even worse than the BOI controlled ICPs. Though BOI has supplied the required technical items 
e.g. computers, PRMs, Webcams etc. at these Seaports ICPs, the space and infrastructure provided 
to the Immigration authorities is grossly inadequate. 
 
4.9.14 Further, in recent past there have been strong pressure by Port Trusts and Tour Operators on 
BOI to perform Immigration function at berths, where no leased line connectivity exists, which is 
non-negotiable as without data flow from main server located at Delhi, no security vetting and 
immigration clearance can be done. 
 
4.9.15 While the airport sector has largely upgraded its infrastructure leading to smoother and 
faster immigration clearance and average traffic growth is of 8-10%, the Ports have not ke pt pace 
with the infrastructure requirements in the face of potential traffic growth. The Shipping industry 
i.e. the Ship Agents/Tour operators are stakeholders just like airlines and they need to invest in 
infrastructure development. Earlier, Ports were charging uniform tariff as decided by Tariff 
Authority for major ports on the basis of GRT of the vessels. However, now there is no uniformity 
of tariff charged at some Ports. 
 
4.9.16 The attention of Ministry of Port, Shipping and Waterways attention has been drawn to the 
infrastructure situation of these 31 sea-ports. There is an urgent need to plug the infrastructure gaps.  
 
4.9.17 The Committee notes that no significant improvement has been made in cre ation of 
adequate infrastructure at the Marine Immigration Posts. The Committee is astonished to 
note that the majority of the Marine Immigration Posts lack basic infrastructure facilities like 
building space, electricity/water connections, monitoring screens, canopy, etc. The Com mittee 
is aware that Bureau of Immigration requires closed climate controlled environment along 
with leased line connectivity to conduct its operations. But in some places, Immigration 
Authorities are working in temporary structures and putting health of officials deployed 
there at risk as also exposing technical gadgets to inclement weather.  

51 
 
 
4.9.18 The Committee observes that this lack of infrastructure facilities is adversely affecting 
the immigration procedure of the incoming passengers. The Committee recommends that the 
MHA should take up the matter on a priority basis at highest level with the Ministry of 
Shipping and various Port Trusts and other concerned agencies to remove the b ottlenecks 
that are hampering the infrastructure development and speed up the development of  desired 
infrastructures at the Marine Immigration Posts. A time-bound framework should also be 
prepared with projected outlays to complete the projects in the Marine Immigration P osts. 
The Committee also recommends for Standarization of infrastructure at all Marine 
Immigration Posts to create uniformity. The Committee is of the considered view that the 
MHA should make adequate budgetary provision in order to support them. The Commi ttee 
also desires to be apprised about the progress made in this regard.

the process of installation of electronic gates at Delhi and Mumbai airport and  on successf ul 
completion the electronic gates may be installed at other airports that are receiving large foot 
falls of foreign travelers. The Committee notes that to get immigration clearance at the “e -
gates”, passengers will need to scan their passports and boarding passes themselves. But, 
without proper awareness, the facility could lead to congestion at the gates instead. The 
Committee, therefore, recommends that the airport staff should provide guidance  to 
passengers regarding the new process and instructions with regards to the use of electronic 
gates may be displayed on screens at important visible locations of the airport to ensure swift 
clearance of passengers at these gates. The additional funds, if required, may be sought at the 
RE stage for this purpose.

4.11.2 The Committee sought to know about the reason for reduction in allocation under this head 
from Rs. 462.55 crore in BE 2020-21 to Rs. 216.50 crore in BE 2021-22. The MHA replied that the

affected due to this situation till December, 2020 (i.e. first three quarters of FY 2020-21). Due to 
this pandemic, very little progress in completion of the ongoing projects could be made having 
impact on the supply of labour& material and mandatory quarantine conditions etc. Further, due to 
lockdown, fund to the tune of Rs. 118.18 crore only was utilized by the LPAI, till 31 st December, 
2020. 
Observation/Recommendation 
4.11.3 The Committee observes that due to COVID-19 Pandemic, LPAI could utilize only Rs.

However, in BE 2021-22, an amount of Rs. 216.50 has been allocated which is almost half of 
the allocation made in BE 2020-21. The Committee, strongly recommends, that this reduc ed

for supply of Cryptographic materials to CAPFs. 
 
(c) Charges paid to other Government Departments: Funds are utilized for reimbursement of 
bills on account of deployment of RPF/ RPSF Companies and State Armed Police Forces 
for election duties. Major chunk of the fund is required for reimbursement to Ministry of 
Railways. 
 
(d) Training and Development: For imparting training on various issues relating to Accounting 
procedures (like New Pension Scheme) to Accounts Officers and principles and practice of 
Public Financial Management. 
 
(e) Infrastructure e-Governance: To maintain E-office package and other related E-Governance 
issues. It was envisioned that all staff who have access to and need to use computer for their 
office work are provided with adequate training.

4.12.4 Besides, the Swatantrata Sainik Samman Yojana was started in the year 1980 to honour and 
grant pension to the Freedom Fighters who took part in the freedom struggle of India. As per the 
provisions of this Scheme, after death of the freedom fighter pensioner, his/her spouse (widow/ 
widower) and after death of spouse, his/her unmarried & unemployed daughters (up to maximum of 
three such daughters at any point of time) and thereafter, mother or father of the freedom fighter 
pensioner are granted dependent family pension. Therefore, stopping the scheme at this juncture 
would not be possible.

4.12.7The Committee takes note of the Welfare Grant provided for payment of Ex- gratia to 
the Next of Kin (NoK) of the deceased CAPFs, AR & J&K Police personnel killed while  on 
duty. The Committee recommends that a portal may be developed for facil itating quick 
disbursement of pension to the families of martyred CAPF personnel.  The Committee notes 
the welfare measures that are being taken to support the livelihood of widow of the martyred 
CAPFs personnel. But, the current welfare measures are interim in nature an d will not be 
able to provide long term relief so as to address socio-economic hardships faced by the 
widows. The Committee, therefore, recommends that the MHA may take up this matter with 
concerned agencies to arrange for basic education, skill development and reser vation in jobs 
as a welfare measure for the widows of the martyred personnel. The Committee strongly 
recommends for the counseling of the family of martyr to reduce emotional stress of losing 
their loved one in the line of duty and extend necessary support to them. The Committee also 
recommends that the MHA should study the aspect of socio-economic hardships faced by the 
parents of the martyred personnel and may also consider to make necessary provisions so 
that a share of pension will also be given to the parents.

4.14.1 Recognising the increasing need for high quality trained manpower in various wings of 
policing, criminal justice and correctional administration, the Government has set up a National 
Police University by the name of Rashtriya Raksha University (RRU). The University, which is an 
institution of National Importance, has commenced its operations from 1 st of October, 2020. RRU 
is a teaching, research and affiliating University, and may affiliate colleges and other institutions in 
States / Union Territories as required. This would meet the country‟s requirements for a pool of 
trained professionals with specialized knowledge and new skill sets in various wings of policing, 
criminal justice and correctional administration. The University will develop synergies with private 
sector by leveraging knowledge and resources from the industry and would also set up Centres of 
Excellence in various fields relating to police and security. 
 
4.14.2 The Committee appreciates the efforts of MHA to open Rashtriya Raksha University 
and National Forensic Sciences University.  The Committee is of the view that  the university 
will provide advance training in sync with the international benchmarks, research, extension 
and education on police science, cyber security and other security related aspe cts which will 
certainly help in strengthening the policing and internal security mechanism in the country. 
The National Forensic Sciences University will play an important role in the arena of 
research and development and training of personnel in forensic science and  relat ed areas 
which will help in strengthening  the investigative machinery and criminal justice de livery 
system. 
4.15 Modernization of Police Forces   
 
4.15.1 The Scheme seeks to bring under its fold many smaller schemes primarily aimed at 
facilitating and assisting the  various  State  Police  Forces, both in terms of finances and 
infrastructure up-gradation, in their efforts in combating various kinds of criminal activities, 
counter-insurgency  operations, etc. 
  
4.15.2 The Details of allocation made for the Modernization of Police Forces are as under: 
 
Table 4.21 
(Rs. in crore) 
 Actuals 
2019-20 
BE 
2020-21 
RE 
2020-21 
BE 
2021-22 
Revenue 4636.95 3161.91 1863.71 2803.11 
 

56 
 
4.15.3 The Government of India, on 27 th September, 2017, approved implementation of umbrella 
scheme of “Modernization of Police Forces(MPF)” for three years‟ period from 2017 -18 to 2019-
20 with the total outlay of Rs. 25,061 crore which include central budget outlay of Rs. 18,636.30 
crore. As of now, the scheme is upto 31.3.2021.The erstwhile scheme of Modernization of Police 
Forces, which had approval up to 2016-17, has been continued beyond 31 st March, 2017, with an 
outlay of Rs. 7,380 crore (@ Rs. 2,460 crore per year) with the new name “Assi stance to States for 
Modernization of Police” under this umbrella scheme. The provision for the Umbrella Scheme 
„Modernization of Police Forces of  States and UTs‟ comprises allocations for the following sub- 
schemes/components: 
 
(a) Cyber Crime Tracking Network System (CCTNS); 
(b) Implementations of e-Prisons; 
(c) Assistance to States for Modernization of Police; 
(d) Assistance to States for Special Projects/Programmes for upgrading police 
infrastructure; 
(e) Assistance to Central Agencies for LWE Management; 
(f) Civic Action Plan (CAP); 
(g) Media Plan (Advertising and Publicity); 
(h) Special Central Assistance (SCA) to 35 worst affected LWE districts; 
(i) Security Related   Expenditure (SRE) [LWE  – Central and State Sectors]; 
(j) SRE (J&K):Relief and Rehabilitation; 
(k) SRE (J&K): Security Environment; 
(l) SRE (J&K): Police; 
(m) SRE (NE); 
(n) Special Infrastructure Scheme (SIS) along with construction of 250 fortified Police 
Stations in LWE affected States; and 
(o) Reimbursement to States for administration of Central Acts and Regulations 
 
4.15.4 The following modifications have been made in the sub-scheme to rectify the shortcomings 
of the previous Scheme: 
 
i. Under this sub- scheme, central assistance has been continued to be provided to all the State 
Governments for weapons, megacity policing and equipment for Forensics, Information 
Technology, Communication, Training, etc. However, to facilitate targeted interventions, 
the earlier scheme was modified so as and activities of „mobility‟ and „construction of 
police infrastructure including housing‟ hav e been allowed for specific theatres viz. Jammu 
& Kashmir (which was covered under the scheme till 31th October, 2019), insurgency 
affected areas of North East(NE) Region and Left Wing Extremism(LWE) affected areas. 
Further, the items under „mobility‟ head  would be used only for strengthening field level 
police offices and not state level police offices. Ceiling of 25% on mobility was also 
introduced. 
  
ii. 20% of the allocation are to be awarded to States based on their performance in the police 
reforms  agenda decided for the year. Earlier there was no such provision.  
 
iii. Calendar for approval of the SAPs have been advanced so that these Plans are approved 
before commencement of the relevant financial year. 

57 
 
 
iv. States have been asked to submit State Security Plan (SSP) based on perspective/strategic 
planning. On the basis of SSP, SAPs are to be formulated.  
v. Process to allow modification up to 25% of total SAP size was simplified. 
 
vi. The mandatory earmarking of funds for procurement from OFB has been omitted 
 
The Committee was informed that the  General Financial Rules 2017 mandates, inter alia 
that before releasing funds to the State Governments under this scheme, it must be ensured 
that they have the capacity to actually spend the balance from the previous year and releases 
made during the current year. As substantial funds were unspent with the States‟ 
implementing agencies, no funds out of allocation at BE stage could be released. This 
financial discipline has specifically become necessary in view of the economic impact of 
COVID-19 global pandemic. Therefore, the following measures have been taken from time 
to time to enhance     utilisation of funds by the States- 
 
(i) „Better performance incentives‟ are awarded to States based on full utilisation of funds. 
Funds remaining unutilized by States are pooled together and distributed to the better 
performing States. This acts as a strong incentive to the States for faster utilization of funds. 
States which have performed better have received funds multiple times their allocation. 
 
(ii) Approval cycle has been advanced so that HPC approved SAPs are available to States much 
before the commencement of financial year. This has a positive effect on procurement cycle. 
Now states have more time to implement projects and submit utilisation certificates. In 
2019-20, fund was released to States in the month of April, 2019 itself; much earlier than 
previous years. 
 
(iii) State Level Empowered Committee (SLEC) has been reconstituted with the State Home 
Secretary heading the Committee in place of Chief Secretary earlier thereby saving  time for 
approval of proposals at State level. On account of any reason, if the State wants change of 
items approved in annual State Action Plan, approval in this regard is how given at the level 
of Chairman of High Powered Committee itself rather than approval of the HPC. This 
reduces time required for such modification resulting in early utilisation of funds. 
 
(iv) States have now prepared long term State Security Plans (3 to 5 years) keeping in mind 
their challenges and requirements. Due to this exercise it is easy for states to chalk out their 
annual actions plans. 
 
(v) In the meetings of High Powered Committee, States have been advised from time to time 
that for the procurement of BP Jacket, various weapons etc., the State Police may tag its 
requirements with ongoing procurement of the CAPFs. This achieves economy of scale as 
well as efficiency of procurements. This is especially beneficial in case of Police Forces of 
smaller States. 
 
The following measures have been taken to provide modern equipment for State police 
forces: 
 
(i) „Police‟ and  „Public Order‟ are State subjects  under the seventh Schedule to the 
Constitution of India. Modernization of police forces is a continuous and ongoing process. 
However, the Government of India supplements the efforts of the State Governments 

58 
 
towards equipping and modernizing of their police forces, under the scheme of „Assistance 
to States for Modernisation of Police‟ [erstwhi le scheme of Modernisation of Police Forces 
(MPF)]. Under this scheme, State Governments are provided central assistance for 
acquiring advanced weaponry such as Assault Rifles, all types of surveillance equipment 
including Unmanned Aerial Vehicles (UAVs), Night Vision Devices (NVDs), CCTV 
surveillance systems and body worn camera systems, modern communication equipment 
and state-of-the-art equipment for Intelligence branches &forensic science laboratories. 
(ii) Under the scheme of Assistance to States for Modernisation of Police (ASMP), the State 
Governments formulate State Action Plan (SAPs) as per their requirements/strategic 
priorities, High Powered Committee approves these proposals. The HPC, which is chaired 
by Divisional Head of Police Modernisation Division and representatives of Financial 
Adviser (Home) and Director General, Bureau of Police Research and Development 
(BPR&D) as members. The Committee is also assisted by experts/resource persons from 
various Central level bodies such as Directorate of Forensic Science Services (DFSS), 
Directorate of Coordination Police Communication (DCPW), National Security Guard 
(NSG), Central Reserve Police Force (CRPF), National Crime Records Bureau (NCRB) etc. 
Therefore, although flexibility of formulating SAPs has been provided to States, it is 
ensured that the States purchase appropriate items. 
(iii) BPR&D publishes compendium of equipment procured by State and UT Police Forces. 
Recently, BPR&D has published such Compendium for the period April, 2014 to March, 
2019. This also serves as reference document for all police forces. 
(iv) „Police‟ being State subject, the State police forces work under their respective State  
Governments. However, Ministry of Home Affairs has role of hand holding the State Police 
Forces on various common modernizations needs of these forces with guidance regarding 
appropriate technologies/equipments, apart from providing assistance to these Forces. 
Adoption of CAPFs‟ Qualitative Requirements & Trial Directives by  State Police Forces or 
circulation of SOPs in various areas of police functions are some of the examples of such 
knowledge sharing. This facilitates additional avenues of guidance for Police Forces, 
especially Police Forces of smaller States. This also avoids duplication of efforts and partly 
removes constraints emanating from the fact that „Police‟ and „Public  Order‟ are State 
subjects. 
(v) It has been experienced that different State Police Forces are at different levels of 
modernization in each of the components. Therefore, it is necessary to set a minimum 
essential benchmark in each of the components so as to bring the States which are lagging 
behind, to the minimum expected technological advancement. In this direction, MHA has 
circulated national standards in the field of forensics, K-9 & radio communication. 
 
4.15.5 Regarding under-utilization of funds by State Governments, the Committee was informed 
by the Home Secretary that at times even when States have  utilized the funds, there is dela yed 
submission of utilization certificates by them. Further, the utilization of funds by some States is 
also poor which is one of the reason for under-utilization of the funds under Modernisation of 
Police Forces.   
4.15.6 The Committee in the meeting of the Committee held on 16th February, 2021 sought to 
know the reasons for the use of body armour, big steel sticks and rods in place of lathis by Delhi 
Police in recent past. The Committee was of the view that it was not modernisation but regression 
and it was flashed across the country which is shocking.   The Home Secretary replied that the use 
of body armour, steel sticks etc. was purely a local level decision and there was no Government 
authorization even by senior officers of the Police Department of Delhi for the same.   

59 
 
4.15.7 The Committee observes that modernization of Police forces is the most urgent need of 
the hour. Therefore, it needs to be expeditiously taken up with a stipulated timeline. The  
Committee recommends that adequate funds must be allocated for purchasin g weaponry, 
bullet proof vehicles, jammers, bomb detection  equipments, communication gadge ts like 
wireless phones, etc., so that the Police personnel are well equipped to deal with the wor st 
situations like live mines on roads, etc. The Committee also recommends for stand ardization 
of Police vehicles all across the country as vehicles carry prestige for the Police. The 
Committee further recommends that the Ministry may ensure the availability of required 
funds as requested by the State Police Department without any procedural de lay of 
sanctioning of funds by the State Government to the Police Department. 
4.15.8 The Committee observes that providing adequate training is integral to modernization 
of Police forces. The Committee strongly believes that training programmes should focus on 
changing the mental attitude and sensitize Police personnel on various issues.  The Committee 
recommends that appropriate budgetary allocation should be made for training and 
sensitization of Police personnel. The Committee also recommends that it should be ensured 
that each and every Police personnel completes full training and under-training personnel 
particularly constables should not be posted for field duties for long. Special incentives may 
also be provided to rope in and attract quality trainers for Police personnel. 
4.16 Crime and Criminal Tracking Network and Systems  (CCTNS) 
4.16.1 The Committee was informed by MHA that a total of Rs.48.03 crore is the allocation for 
Crime and Criminal Tracking Network & Systems (CCTNS) in BE 2021-22.Against a target of 
covering 14,306 police stations, CCTNS has been deployed in 15,773 Police Stations out of 16,156 
police stations in the country. 100% FIRs are being recorded in 15,568 Police Stations on CCTNS. 
All States/UTs have launched their State Citizen Portals providing police services and a total of 
6.15 crore citizen service requests have been handled. 
 
4.16.2 The Committee was informed by the Administration of UT of Lakshadweep that the 
primary challenge in island comes from the SWAN PoPs to CCTNS sites (Police Station).  Even if 
the islands of Lakshdweep have increased bandwidth and all of it is directed to the CCTNS 
locations, the present BSNL Copper Based Lines would not be able to provide proper bandwidth 
for CCTNS to operate properly.    
4.17 e-FIR 
 
4.17.1 Regarding registration of e-FIR, the Committee was informed that the facility for onli ne 
filing of e-FIR has commenced in Odisha in addition to Delhi, Uttar Pradesh and Rajasthan. e-FIR 
is available for registering cases on property and vehicle thefts, and also in cases where ac cused 
details are unknown. Since „Police‟ and  „Public order‟ are State subjects under the Seventh 
Schedule to the Constitution of India, and States/UTs have to implement e-FIR in their respective 
States/UTs. In order to facilitate them, the National Crime Records Bureau has shared an e- FIR 
module in CCTNS with the States/UTs. NCRB has also advised the States/UTs to customize, if 
required, and integrate the e-FIR module with State CCTNS application. Till date, a total of 8.05 
lakh e-FIRs have been registered in these four States (Delhi – 802301, Odisha – 331, Rajasthan – 
88 and Uttar Pradesh – 3164). 
 
4.17.2 The Committee notes that CCTNS has been deployed in 15,773 police stations out of 
16,156 police stations in the country. The Committee observes that for effective functioning of 
CCTNS all across the country, good connectivity of internet is needed. But, the internet 
connectivity in North-Eastern States and in UT of Lakshdweep, etc., is not adequate . 

60 
 
Therefore, the Committee believes that not only CCTNS but also various other 
schemes/programmes/services which are running through online mode will not be mu ch 
effective unless good internet connectivity is available all across the country. 
4.17.3 The Committee notes that the facility for online filing of e-FIR has co mmenced in 
Delhi, Uttar Pradesh, Rajasthan and Odisha. The Committee further recommends that the 
MHA should coordinate with State Police and bring more States/ on board for on line 
registration of cases to facilitate the registration of FIR from anywhere so that investigation 
can begin without any delay.

borders till November, 2020. The Committee, recommends to speed up the constru ction of 
border roads, fencing and floodlighting works in the remaining areas so as to secure  the 
border and strengthen monitoring of illicit movement/trafficking across borders.

A small outlay is allotted to Delhi Police Rs.10.14 crore for their schemes in the national capital

unspent balance lying at credit in the Fund. An amount of Rs.100 crore is proposed to be 
transferred to the Nirbhaya Fund in the Public Account in the year 2021-22. 
 
4.19.2 The Committee sought to know about the projects that are being implemented by MHA 
under the Nirbhaya Fund. The MHA replied that the projects for safety of women being 
implemented by Ministry of Home Affairs under the Nirbhaya Fund scheme include, 112

safety by Delhi Police; Safe City projects in 8 Cities; Strengthening DNA analysis, cyber fo rensic

districts; setting-up of a State-of-the-Art DNA  analysis facility in CFSL Chandigarh; Traini ng and 
Capacity Building of Criminal Justice functionaries.  
 
4.19.3 The Committee was informed by MHA that as per available information, there is a likely 
balance of  Rs. 1,167.91 crore in Nirbhaya Fund corpus in FY 2020-21, which would be available 
for Nirbhaya Fund projects in the FY 2021-22. In order to ensure optimum utilization of corpus 
reserves, the Nirbhaya Fund transfer proposed is Rs.100.00 crore. After taking into account the 
transfer of fund into the Nirbhaya Fund corpus from various Ministries, the projected demand for 
MHA projects under Nirbhaya Fund totaling to Rs. 759.59 crore in FY 2021-22 which could be 
met from the funds available in the corpus in the FY. 
 
4.19.4 A total of Rs. 321.15 crore had been utilized for the 112 National Emergency Response 

63 
 
System (ERSS) till the FY 2019-20, and the total utilization till 31 December 2020 was Rs. 336.52 
crore.  The single number 112 national emergency response service is now operational in 33 
States/UTs in the country and work is underway to complete the project in the remaining 3 States. 
 
4.19.5 The Ministry of Home Affairs (MHA) is implementing Cyber Crime Prevention for 
Women and Children (CCPWC) Scheme at an estimated cost of Rs. 223.198 Crore, to be met from 
Nirbhaya Funds of Ministry of Women & Children Development, for the implementation period 
2017-2020. The Scheme has been extended up to 31 st March, 2021. Further, implementation period 
of the scheme is being proposed to be extended for another two years beyond 31 st March, 2021. 
CIS Division has been able to utilize the funds to the extent of Rs. 112.62 crore up to the close of 
financial year 2019-20, out of the total cost provision of Rs.223.198 crore and the rest amount i.e 
Rs. 110.578 crore shall be utilized in the ensuing financial years 2020-21 to 2022-23.In Cyber 
Crime Prevention against Women and Children, a total of Rs.113.66 crore had been spent till FY 
2019-20 and as on December 2020, this has increased to Rs. 130.85 crore.  
4.19.6 For Strengthening of Forensic Science Laboratories, the Committee was informed that the 
Criminal Law (Amendment) Act, 2018 was enacted to, inter-alia, prescribe even more stringent 
penal provisions. While the law was made stringent, the Government also felt that effective 
deterrence lay not only in stringency of punishment but also in the delivery of the criminal justice. 
This required that the amendments effected in the law are backed up with adequate capacity 
building to ensure timeliness and efficiency in deliverance of criminal justice. In this regard, one 
of the measures which has been taken up is for strengthening DNA analysis, cyber forensic and 
related facilities in State Forensic Science Laboratories. Since „Police‟ and „Public order‟ are State 
subjects as per Seventh Schedule of the Constitution of India, the Ministry undertook a gap analysis 
of the existing facilities through the States/UTs, and basis the demand assessment of each State/UT, 
has sanctioned projects in 20 States/UTs for total cost of Rs.189.45 crore. The remaining 
States/UTs are preparing their projects and the same are proposed to be taken up in the coming 
years. 
4.19.7 The Committee notes that the Ministry has sanctioned projects for strengthening DNA 
analysis, cyber forensic and related facilities in 20 States/UTs for total cost of Rs.189.45 crore. 
The Committee, recommends that the MHA may take up with the rem aining States for 
establishment of forensic and DNA analysis labs within a time frame and make  adequatory 
budgetary allocations for the same. This will help in improving investigating m achinery and 
strengthen criminal justice delivery mechanis m.

people living in remote  and  inaccessible areas situated near the International borders.

4.20.2 The Committee was informed by the MHA that t he projected demand under this head for 
FY 2021-22 was Rs. 839 crore, however, the funds received under BADP is Rs. 565.71.  
 
4.20.3 In the meeting of the Committee held on 16th February, 2021, the Home Secretary 
informed the Committee that the Ministry is concerned towards installation of mobiler towers to 
improve communication and also about the genration of economic activities to provide livelihood 
oppertunities to the people living in border areas. 
 
4.20.4 The Committee was further informed by MHA that the border population plays an 
important role in the overall security of the border areas of the country. They functions as „Eyes 
and Ears‟ of the nation especially the security forces. Further, the border population due to its 
presence in the farthest most areas of the country also helps in staking claim to the areas/territorie s 
under control and defining the sanctity of International Boundary. Hence, the active support of 
border population enhances security of border areas. It is in this context that effective coordination 
between Border Guarding Forces (BGFs), border population and public representatives is essential. 
Public representatives do also help in roping village elders to counsel youth not to indulge in trans-
border crimes. 
 
4.20.5 A „Simant Kshetra Vikasotsav-2020‟ was held in Dhordo Village, Kutch (Gujarat) on 12 th 
November, 2020, as a unique platform to sensitize border population about their role in national 
security and development works initiated by the Government to provide critical infrastructure and 
ensuring sustainable living for them. Village heads, members of district and taluk panchayats of 
158 border villages from three border districts of Gujarat, namely Kutch, Banaskantha and Patan 
districts sharing land borders with Pakistan, attended the programme. 
 
4.20.6 The Committee sought to know about the status of communication infrastructure at Ladakh. 
The MHA replied that as per the information received from M/o Telecommunications, the 
Telecom infrastructure in border villages in Ladakh is as under:

4.20.7 Under one of the Schemes of Universal Service Obligation Fund (USOF), i.e. "354 Village 
Connectivity for border Areas Scheme", 57 villages of Ladakh are being covered. The sch eme is 
under implementation and targeted to be completed by July, 2021. The remaining villages will be 
covered by Department of Telecom and Telecom Service Providers (TSPs) in a phased manner in 
due course. 
 
4.20.8 The BADP guidelines revised in 2020 cover all the census villages/towns, semi-urban and 
urban areas located within 0-10 km distance (crow-fly/aerial distance) from the first habitation 
from International Boundary (IB). The imaginary line joining all the first habitations fr om the IB 
will be the Zero line for BADP and the 10 km distance towards the interior will be calculated from 
this zero line. Once 0-10 km area is saturated, the programme may cover10- 20/30/40/50 KM areas. 
4.20.9 The Committee appreciates the initiative taken by the Ministry of Home Affairs in 
holding the „Simant  Kshetra Vikasotsav- 2020‟ in Dhordo Village, Kutch (Gujarat) on 12 th 

65 
 
November, 2020 aimed at sensitizing border population about their role in national security 
and development works. The Committee observes that as the people living in border areas  
are well versed with the terrain and other geographical conditions they can be  of immense 
help in providing civil intelligence inputs relating to the area. The Committee, therefor e, 
recommends that the MHA should conduct „Simant Kshetra Vikasotsav- 2020‟ from time to 
time in other border areas also. This will make people living in border areas aware of the 
developmental schemes/programmes of the Governments which are aimed at providing 
sustainable living and overall development of border areas. 
 
4.20.10 The Committee notes that the revised  BADP guidelines have been framed in 
the year 2020 which intends to cover 0-50 km area from the Zero line. The Com mittee 
expresses its concern that currently, some  border villages of Ladakh have not been covered  
under the scheme of BADP. Therefore, the Committee recommeds that the MHA take up the 
matter to include all border areas of Ladakh including hamlets and other barren lands with 
small population as per the revised BADP guidelines so that they get the benefits of 
schems/programme under BADP. 
 
4.20.11The Committee notes that out of total 236 habitable villages, 172 villages have telecom 
infrastructure and only 24 and 78 villages have 3G and 4G internet connectivity, respectively, 
which is very low. The Committee understands the importance of proper communicatio n 
facilities including internet has grown considerably over years and has taken center stage  
during the COVID-19 Pandemic when students have been receiving education through onli ne 
mode due to closure of schools. The Committee also recommends to electrify all vil lages in 
Ladakh particularly, the villages that are located in Zero-Border like Chumar and Demchuk 
in order to stop migration of people from these areas. This will go a long way in fulfilling the 
developmental needs of these remote yet strategically important localities.

CHAPTER - V 
 
DEMAND NO. 51 TO 58 - UNION TERRITORIES 
 
5.1 Introduction 
 
5.1.1 The Demands for Grants (Nos. 51-58) relate to the country's eight Union Territories (UTs). Of 
these, five relate to UTs without Legislature, namely Andaman & Nicobar Islands (Demand No. 51), 
Chandigarh (Demand No. 52), Dadra and Nagar Haveli and Daman and Diu (Demand No. 53), 
Ladakh (Demand No.54) and Lakshadweep (Demand No. 55). The remaining three Grants relate to 
UT with Legislatures, Transfer to Delhi (Demand No. 56), Transfer to Jammu & Kashmir (Demand 
No. 57) and Transfer to Puducherry (Demand No. 58). As per Article 280 of the Constitution of India, 
the Finance Commission makes recommendation for distribution of funds between the Union and the 
States. The needs of UTs are fulfilled in the Union Budget. The overall Budget Estimate and 
Expenditure (as on 17 th February, 2021) for the year 2021-22 pertaining to the five UTs without 
legislature have been given in the following table:

5.2.2 The Committee was informed by MHA  that the Andaman & Nicobar Island has witnessed 
appreciable development in the last six years consequent upon the formation of Islands 
Development Authority in 2015.  Some of the major achievements in Andaman inter-alia includes 
undersea OFC that has connected Chennai to Port Blair and seven other islands.   It has provided 
high-speed broadband connectivity to the islanders. Further, 381 lakh sq. kms. of Exclusive 
Economic Zone has been released from „No Go Zone‟ for oil exploration. Efforts have also been 
made to promote green energy in these islands and 24.77 MW green energy has been added. Thes e 
islands, largely, have been using diesel.  To enhance road connectivity, double laning of 330 kms of 
Andaman Trunk Road and a bridge across Humphrey Strait has been taken up. The project is in 
advance stage of construction.  Two 500 passenger vessels are also under final stages of 
construction. This will add further connectivity in these islands.   

69 
 
Cellular Jail  
5.2.3 The Committee sought to know about the steps taken to document the historiography of the 
freedom fighters who were imprisoned in Cellular Jail. The representative of UT of A&N Islands 
informed the Committee that the cellular jail also called Kala Paani was the land of no return many 
freedom fighters have suffered there.  While many of the freedom fighters have been documented, 
historiography definitely needs to look into various others who were kept in the cellular jail but 
went unnoticed.   
5.2.4 The Committee was further informed that the National Memorial Cellular Jail is a mute 
witness to the untold sufferings and sacrifices of our freedom fighters who laid down their lives for 
the cause of their motherland. A&N Administration, is committed to preserving India's heritage, so 
far the following actions has taken:- 
 
a. The Central Tower of the Cellular Jail has 18 marble plaques on the walls depicting the 
names of 513 freedom fighters incarcerated in the Cellular Jail from 1909-1938 . 
 
b. A Freedom Fighter‟s P hoto Gallery has been set up in the Administrative Block of the 
National Memorial Cellular Jail.The brief write-ups of revolutionary cases with available 
photographs of all Freedom Fighters incarcerated in the Cellular Jail are on display in the 
Gallery. 
 
c. The Department of Art & Culture, A&N Administration has made an effort to compile the 
brief profile of all freedom fighters in the form of a book “Unsung Heroes of Freedom 
Struggle in Andamans”- who were deported to the penal settlement in Andamans after the 
first war of independence in 1857 and revolutionary freedom fighters incarcerated in the 
Cellular Jail during 1909-1938. Necessary action is being taken to collect details of 
freedom fighters deported to the Cellular Jail from various State Archives. 
 
5.2.5 The Committee notes the efforts made by the Administration of UT of           A&N 
Islands for documentation of freedom fighters. The Committee recommend s that a 
biographical sketch may be created of all freedom fighters who were imprisoned  in cellula r 
jail during the course of freedom struggle so as to acquaint the incoming tourists of the 
history associated with the freedom struggle. 
Integration of tribals in the mainstream
 
 
5.2.6 Regarding the welfare of tribal population and plan of the UT administration to integrate 
them, the Committee was informed that interventions have been made to prolong the longevity of 
tribals and bring improvement in their health and basic things without forcing them. As a result, the 
population of Onges has gone up which was earlier dwindling down.  The Andaman and Nicobar 
Tribal Research and Training Institute (ANTRI) have submitted a proposal before the Tribal 
Welfare Ministry about the integration of tribals to the mainstream of development.     
5.2.7 There are six Scheduled Tribes i.e., the Andamanese, Onges, Jarawas, Sentinelese, Nicobarese 
and Shompens notified by the President of India under Article 342 (1) of the Indian Constitution vide  
the Constitution (Andaman & Nicobar Islands) Scheduled Tribes Order, dated 31 st March, 1959. Except 
Nicobarese the rest of the 05 tribes are categorized into Particularly Vulnerable Tribal Groups (PVTGs).  
 
5.2.8 The tribes of A&N Islands are in different stages of socio-economic condition. The Nicobarese 
have joined the mainstream and the Sentinlese are still in isolation practicing primordial hunting and 
gathering way of life.  The other tribes namely Jarawas, Onges, Andamanese and Shompens are partly 

70 
 
in contact with the outside population, but they prefer to remain in their own abode, particularly 
Shompens, Sentinelese and the Jarawas.   
 
5.2.9 As furnished by the MHA, the decade-wise estimated tribal population is given below: -

5.2.10 With regard to inclusion in the mainstream economic and social life, the Committee was 
informed that the Administration is granting maximum autonomy to the PVTGs especially Jarawas 
and Shompens with minimum and regulated intervention and that there shall be no intervention in 
their cultural life and they will be left at liberty to develop according to their own genius and at 
their own pace. The Administration is not making any attempt to bring them to the mainstream 
society against their conscious will.  
5.2.11 In order to protect and preserve the culture of the Tribals in A&N Islands, the UT 
Administration has established two societies under Societies Registration Act 1860 namely (i) 
Andaman Adim Janjati Vikas Samiti (AAJVS) and (ii) A&N Tribal Research and Training Institute 
(ANTRI), which conceptualizes and implements various welfare measures/ policies with regards to 
Particularly Vulnerable tribal Groups (PVTGs). In case of the Nicobari tribe, which is the only non-
PVTG tribe in A&N Islands, the developmental initiatives are implemented by Integrated Tribal 
Development Agency.  
 
5.2.12 The representative of UT of A&N Islands informed the Committee that the islands have 
been opened up for Restricted Area Permit (RAP).  But as far as tourist areas are concerned, apa rt 
from conducting tribal research, people are not allowed to visit the tribal areas. 
5.2.13 The Committee is anguished to note that no expenditure was made under „Welfare of 
Tribal  and Other Backward Classes‟  in year 2020 -21 (till 31 st December, 2020) against the 
allocated amount of 0.76 crore in BE 2020-21. The Committee observes that without utilization  
of funds how the UT Administration is going to conserve the vulnerable tribal groups in the UT. 
The Committee recommends that budgetary provisions should be fruitfully utilized  for welfare 
and development of tribals particularly PVTGs  for conservation of their tradition, culture, 
dialects, languages, etc. The Committee also recommends that stritct vigilence should be put in 
place to ensure that the tourists do not venture into the tribal areas and harm the tribals in any 
way. 

71 
 
New Greenfield Airport at Port Blair 
5.2.14 Regarding air connectivity with the mainland, the Committee was informed that high air 
fare in mainland-islands sector especially during peak tourist season is a major concern in case of 
the local residents of A & N Islands. Therefore, capping of Air fare by the Ministry of Civil 
Aviation will benefit the Islander especially the weaker section while travelling on mainland-island 
sector at affordable fare. Further, due to operational limitations of the existing VSI Airport at Port  
Blair, an alternate Greenfield airport has been proposed to be developed at Port Blair for which 
suitable site has been identified by AAI. Once ready, this facility will help to ensure 24x7 
connectivity of the island with mainland. 
 
5.2.15 The Committee notes that an alternate Greenfield airport has been proposed to be 
developed at Port Blair to improve connectivity with the mainland. The Committee is aware 
of the ecological sensitivity and strategic sensitivity of the region and is of the view that 
islands don't need large airports or big planes as it will be damaging to the environment. 
Instead, the small planes with carrying capacity of 50-70 passengers are adequate to cater to 
the need of tourists and local people.  The Committee, therefore, recommends that the Home 
Ministry may convene a meeting of all the concerned Ministries/Departments to encourage 
use of small planes in A & N Islands which is sustainable.
